<#sep#>user: I am trying to write a song with lyrics by Plato (the Greek philosopher). Give me a cord progression that Greeks loved to use and write philosophical lyrics. The song will be played on the piano and a sad melancholic singer is going to provide the vocals.

assistant: For the chord progression, a commonly used one in traditional Greek music is the Dorian mode. The Dorian mode is a type of musical scale with a sad and melancholic character, making it a good choice for the mood of your song. A simple Dorian chord progression that you could use for your song might look like this:

| C | D | E flat | F | G | A flat | B flat | C |
| Dorian | Dorian | Phrygian | Dorian | Dorian | Aeolian | Locrian | Dorian |

For the lyrics, you could consider using quotes or themes from Plato's works that reflect his philosophical views. Some potential sources for lyrics could include "The Republic," "The Symposium," or "The Phaedo." Here is an example of a verse and chorus inspired by Plato's philosophy:

Verse 1:
"In this world of shadows, what is real?
A journey to truth, that's what I feel
The Forms above, they guide me home
Where knowledge and beauty are never alone."

Chorus:
"Oh, philosophy, my guiding light
In this world of darkness, you make everything right
Through the trials and tribulations, you show me the way
To the truth, to the beauty, to the eternal day."

This is just one example of how you could incorporate Plato's philosophy into the lyrics of your song. Feel free to use these lyrics as a starting point or write your own based on Plato's works and ideas.

user: I want to give the song a more authentic Greek feel. What are some instruments that would have been used when Plato was alive and where might I be able to buy them? Also are there any other stylistic quirks or oddities about ancient Greek music that might make the song sound more unique?

assistant: Some authentic instruments from ancient Greece that you could use in your song include:

- Aulos - a double-reed instrument similar to a oboe, often used to accompany singing or poetry recitals
- Lyre - a stringed instrument similar to a small harp, considered a noble instrument and used in education
- Kithara - a larger stringed instrument, similar to a lyre butwith thicker strings and a deeper sound

These instruments can be purchased from specialty music stores or luthiers that create replicas of ancient instruments. Some other characteristics of ancient Greek music to incorporate:

- Use of modes like Dorian, Phrygian, and Mixolydian which have distinctive sounds
- Focuses on melody rather than harmony, with instrumental accompaniment and solo sections featuring virtuosic playing
- Lyrics were an important part, with poetry played an integral role in performance
- Rhythms tended to be relatively simple, based on poetic meter

Incorporating some of these elements into your song can help give it a more authentic Greek feel and reflect the music of Plato's time. The melody, modes, and instrumental accompaniment in particular can help capture the melancholy and philosophical character you described.<#sep#>-

import sys

def main():
    n, k = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())
    a = list(map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split()))
    
    sorted_a = sorted(a)
    a = [0] + a  # 1-based indexing
    sorted_a = [0] + sorted_a  # 1-based indexing
    
    INF = float('inf')
    dp = [[INF] * (k + 1) for _ in range(n + 1)]
    dp[0][0] = 0
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        for j in range(0, k + 1):
            if j > 0 and a[i] == sorted_a[i]:
                dp[i][j] = dp[i-1][j-1]
            if i > 1:
                cost = (sorted_a[i] - sorted_a[i-1]) ** 2
                if dp[i-1][j] != INF:
                    dp[i][j] = min(dp[i][j], dp[i-1][j] + cost)
            if j > 0 and i > 1 and a[i] == sorted_a[i]:
                cost = (sorted_a[i] - sorted_a[i-1]) ** 2
                if dp[i-1][j-1] != INF:
                    dp[i][j] = min(dp[i][j], dp[i-1][j-1] + cost)
    
    print(dp[n][k])

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()<#sep#> 1. For each fixed k, as n approaches infinity, |sin(π k! x)|^(1/n) approaches 1. However, when k is varying up to n, as n increases, the number of terms in the sum increases. So we have sum_{k=1}^n a_k(n), where a_k(n) approaches 1 as n approaches infinity for each fixed k. But as n increases, the sum is of n terms each approaching 1, so the sum would approach n, which goes to infinity. Therefore, for irrational x, the limit would be infinity, which is unbounded.

Wait, but maybe some terms are approaching 1 very slowly? Let me verify that. Let's take a fixed x irrational. Then for each k, |sin(π k! x)| is some number in (0,1). Then |sin(π k! x)|^(1/n) = exp( (1/n) * ln |sin(π k! x)| ). As n approaches infinity, (1/n) * ln |sin(π k! x)| approaches 0, since ln |sin(π k! x)| is a negative number (because |sin(...)| < 1), so exp(0) = 1. Therefore, each term approaches 1. But the sum is from k=1 to n, which is n terms each approaching 1. So the sum would be approximately n * 1, which goes to infinity. Hence, the limit would be infinity. Therefore, for irrational x, the limit is unbounded. Therefore, the conclusion is that the limit is bounded if and only if x is rational.

Wait, but let me check with an example. Let's take x = 1, which is rational. Then x = 1/1, so q = 1. Then for k >= 1, k! x = k! * 1, which is integer. Therefore, for k >=1, |sin(π k! x)| = 0. So the sum from k=1 to n would be sum_{k=1}^n 0^{1/n}. Wait, but 0^{1/n} is 0 for n >=1. Therefore, the sum is 0 for all n. Therefore, the limit is 0, which is bounded. So x=1, a rational number, gives a bounded limit.

Another example: x = 1/2. Then q = 2. For k >=2, k! x = k!*(1/2) = (k!)/2. Since k! is divisible by 2 for k >=2, so k! x is integer. Therefore, for k >=2, |sin(π k! x)| = 0. Therefore, the sum from k=1 to n is |sin(π 1! x)|^(1/n) + sum_{k=2}^n 0. So |sin(π * (1/2))|^(1/n) = |sin(π/2)|^(1/n) = 1^(1/n) = 1. So the sum is 1 for all n >=1. Therefore, the limit is 1, which is bounded.

Another example: x = 1/3. Then q =3. For k >=3, k! x = k! /3. Since 3 divides k! for k >=3, so k! x is integer. Therefore, for k >=3, |sin(π k! x)| = 0. So the sum from k=1 to n would be |sin(π *1! x)|^(1/n) + |sin(π *2! x)|^(1/n) + sum_{k=3}^n 0. Let's compute these terms. For k=1: |sin(π*(1/3))| = |sin(π/3)| = sqrt(3)/2. For k=2: 2! x = 2*(1/3) = 2/3. |sin(2π/3)| = sqrt(3)/2. So both terms are sqrt(3)/2. Therefore, the sum becomes (sqrt(3)/2)^(1/n) + (sqrt(3)/2)^(1/n). As n approaches infinity, each term (sqrt(3)/2)^(1/n) approaches 1. Therefore, the sum approaches 2. So the limit is 2, which is bounded.

Now, take an irrational x, like sqrt(2). For each k, k! x is not an integer, so |sin(π k! x)| is in (0,1). Then each term |sin(π k! x)|^(1/n) approaches 1 as n approaches infinity. The sum from k=1 to n is then n terms each approaching 1, so the sum tends to infinity. Therefore, the limit is unbounded.

Therefore, it seems that the set of x where the limit is bounded is exactly the rational numbers. So the answer should be all x in Q, the rational numbers.

Wait, but let me check another example where x is rational with a denominator that is not prime. For example, x=1/4. Then q=4. For k >=4, k! x = k!/4, which is integer since 4 divides k! for k >=4. So the sum from k=1 to n is sum_{k=1}^3 |sin(π k! x)|^(1/n) + sum_{k=4}^n 0. So for k=1: |sin(π*(1/4))|=sin(π/4)=sqrt(2)/2≈0.707. For k=2: 2! x = 2/4 = 1/2, so |sin(π/2)|=1. For k=3: 3! x=6/4=3/2, |sin(3π/2)|=1. Therefore, the sum is (sqrt(2)/2)^(1/n) + 1^(1/n) + 1^(1/n). As n→∞, each term approaches 1, so the sum approaches 1 + 1 + 1 = 3. So the limit is 3, which is bounded. So yes, for x=1/4, it works.

But what if x=0? Then x=0, which is 0/1, so q=1. For k >=1, k! x =0, which is integer. So all terms are |sin(0)|=0. Therefore, the sum is 0 for all n, so the limit is 0, which is bounded. So x=0 is included as well.

Another edge case: x=1/6. Then q=6. For k >=3, since 6 divides 3! =6. So k! x =k!/6. For k >=3, k! x is integer, so terms from k=3 onwards are 0. The sum would be for k=1: |sin(π/6)|=1/2, k=2: |sin(2π/6)|=|sin(π/3)|=sqrt(3)/2. So the sum is (1/2)^(1/n) + (sqrt(3)/2)^(1/n). As n→∞, each term approaches 1, so the sum approaches 2. So the limit is 2, which is bounded.

So in all these examples, rational x give bounded limits. Therefore, it's convincing that rational numbers are exactly the ones where the limit is bounded. For irrational x, since the terms never become zero, and each term approaches 1, the sum goes to infinity. Therefore, the answer should be all rational numbers x ∈ Q.

But just to confirm, let me think if there's any other possibility. Suppose x is a Liouville number, which is a type of transcendental number that can be very well approximated by rationals. But even then, for each k, k! x may not be integer, so |sin(π k! x)| would still be positive. So even for such x, the sum would still go to infinity. So I don't think there are any other x besides rationals where the limit is bounded.

Therefore, the final answer is that all real numbers x that are rational, so x ∈ Q.

 Thus, the optimal way to handle a cycle of k nodes is that for all nodes in the cycle, compute:

Total_time_start = 1/p_i + sum_{ for next nodes in the path} of __的 w's. 

But to compute sum after the starting point:

 The cycle is broken by the starting point, forming a chain. 

 For example, for a 4-cycle: if starting at node k, the path is k → successor → ... etc.

The total would be 1/p_k + 1/w_{successor} +1/w_{next}... 

Thus, for any cycle, the minimal total is the minimum over all nodes i of (1/p_i + sum_{ the remaining nodes in the cycle , assuming you go a_round} (1/ their w values) )

 So, for a cycle of k nodes, let's denote the nodes in order 0,1,…,k-1 such that each i's next is (i+1) mod k. 

 We can compute all possible starting points and choose the best. 

 For example, for the first test case:

 the 4 nodes are arranged as 0 →1 →2 →3 →0.

Thus, the approach is, Foreach cycle, for each starting node among the cycle's nodes, compute the expected time if starting there and going in the optimal path around the cycle.

 We should choose the starting node which gives the minimal total expected time.

 So, to handle cycle components:

1. For each cycle node, compute the cost of starting at node i: cost_i = (1/p_i) + sum over all predecessors in the cyclic path.

   The sum is the total of all nodes after the first in the path, each of which contributes 1/w_j.

   (Note: Each后续 node is the next in the cycle's path, so the weapon from the previous node is availe due to their defeat.)

2. The minimal cost among all nodes' costs is the optimal contribution of that cycle.

 Thus, for a cycle with nodes ordered as vi_0, vi_1,…,vi_{k-1} (in the sequence), then the cost for starting at node vi can be written as:

 cost_start=1/p_vi + sum_{m=1 to k-1} 1/w_{vi_{(m)}}

 but vi_{(m)} 是 starting node's next m-steps in the cycle's path.

Thus, for the first example's cycle of 4, the minimal is 7.277…

Now, moving forward, the plan is:

1. Decompose the graph into its components. Each component is a cycle with trees leading into them.

2. For each tree node (in the functional graph's trees leading into the cycle). We need to determine the optimal path to process them first. 

 How ?

For the trees leading into a cycle:

A functional graph component consists of a cycle and trees feeding into the nodes of the cycle. Each tree is a chain leading to a node in the cycle. 

 A tree node has its id pointing to another node in the component, possibly up the tree until reaching the cycle's nodes.

The optimal path must process the tree nodes before their dependent nodes can be processed efficiently.

```
  File "/home/jenkins/.local/lib/python3.8/site-packages/diff_cover/violationsreporters/violations_reporter.py", line 469, in parse_reports
    violation = Violation(int(line_number), error_str)
ValueError: invalid literal for int() with base 10: '470]'
```

The reason for this is the following: `violations_reporter` expects, that the output for duplicating lines looks like:
```
file1.py:162: [R0801] Similar lines in 2 files
==file1:162
==student.views:4
```
but it can look like:
```
file1.py:162: [R0801] Similar lines in 2 files
==file1:[162:165]
==student.views:[4:7]
```
And the [`multi_line_violation_regex`](https://github.com/Bachmann1234/diff_cover/blob/61a0ae286a54e7fcbacf063d339ddace6fd84155/diff_cover/violationsreporters/violations_reporter.py#L405) regular expression `==(\w|.+):(.*)` cannot parse it correctly.

The correct regular expression is `==((?:\w|\.)+?):\[?(\d+)`.

+++ b/diff_cover/violationsreporters/violations_reporter.py

         # path/to/file.py:123: [C0111] Missing docstring
         # path/to/file.py:456: [C0111, Foo.bar] Missing docstring
-        self.multi_line_violation_regex = re.compile(r"==(\w|.+):(.*)")
+        self.multi_line_violation_regex = re.compile(r"==((?:\w|\.)+?):\[?(\d+)")

<#sep#>user: How do I terminate fiber optic cable?

assistant: You’ll need to prepare: a cleaver, a striper, a cable clamp, a gauge, Kevlar scissors, cleaning supplies and the connector. Now, follow the procedures below to terminate fibers on your own.

1.First, cut and clean the fiber optic cable before you start. Use the clamp to fix the cable, assemble the connector housing, put the boot on the cable before you start stripping it.

2.Measure the jacket strip length with the gauge, use the stripper to strip off the jacket and cut off the Kevlar fibers with the scissors. Measure the buffer strip length and strip the buffer off at about 1-centimeter segments (1 centimeter at a time) to keep the fiber from bending or breaking. Then use the stripper to slightly clean off the cladding.

3.Insert the fiber strand into the trapdoor of the cleaver, fix it with the clamp and start the cleaver. After a precision cleave, you need to decontaminate the dust, grease or the leftovers of the cladding from the fiber. Use the cleaning supplies (alcohol) to draw the contaminants from the fiber and repeat this step several times until it’s clean.

4.Insert the fiber into the pre-assembled fiber connector until you feel the pressure to make sure it’s connected. Then slightly slide the boot up and tighten the connector. Lastly, remove the cable clamp and you got a finished fiber connector.

user: I think I cut my cable too short. Is there any way I can make it longer?

assistant: There are a few ways to lengthen a fiber optic cable, and the method you choose will depend on the type of cable and the desired outcome. Here are a few common methods:

Splicing: This is the most common method for extending the length of a fiber optic cable. It involves removing the protective jacket from the ends of two fiber optic cables and splicing the bare fibers together using a splice tray or fusion splicer. This creates a permanent connection between the two fibers that is strong enough to transmit light signals over the entire length of the combined cables.

Cable extension: Another option is to use fiber optic cable extensions, which are essentially pre-terminated fiber optic cables that can be easily attached to existing fiber optic cables to extend their length. These extensions typically come in a variety of lengths and connector types, making it easy to find one that is compatible with your existing cable.

Using patch cords: A patch cord is a short fiber optic cable that has connectors on both ends. You can use patch cords to extend the length of a fiber optic cable by connecting one end of the patch cord to the existing cable and the other end to a fiber optic component such as a patch panel or switch.

Optical amplifiers: In some cases, you may need to extend the length of a fiber optic cable over a long distance. In these situations, optical amplifiers can be used to regenerate the light signal, allowing it to travel longer distances.

Regardless of the method you choose, it's important to use high-quality components and follow proper installation procedures to ensure that the extended cable meets industry standards and performs optimally.<#sep#>-

import math

n, r, v = map(int, input().split())

for _ in range(n):
    s, f = map(int, input().split())
    delta = f - s
    if delta == 0:
        print(0.0)
        continue
    low = 0.0
    high = (delta + 2 * r) / v  # Initial high estimate
    for _ in range(100):
        mid = (low + high) / 2
        vt_over_2r = (v * mid) / (2 * r)
        current = v * mid - 2 * r * math.sin(vt_over_2r)
        if current >= delta:
            high = mid
        else:
            low = mid
    print(high)<#sep#>, the final answer is that all real numbers x that are rational, so x ∈ Q.
</think>To find all \( x \in \mathbb{R} \) such that the limit
\[ \lim_{n \to \infty} f_n(x) = \lim_{n \to \infty} \sum_{k=1}^{n} |\sin (\pi k! x)|^{1/n} \]
is bounded, we need to analyze the behavior of the terms \( |\sin (\pi k! x)|^{1/n} \) as \( n \) approaches infinity.

### Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Behavior of \( |\sin (\pi k! x)|^{1/n} \) as \( n \to \infty \)**:
   - For a fixed \( k \), \( |\sin (\pi k! x)| \) is a value between 0 and 1.
   - As \( n \to \infty \), \( 1/n \to 0 \).
   - If \( |\sin (\pi k! x)| > 0 \), then \( |\sin (\pi k! x)|^{1/n} \to 1 \).
   - If \( |\sin (\pi k! x)| = 0 \), then \( |\sin (\pi k! x)|^{1/n} = 0 \).

2. **Sum Analysis**:
   - The sum \( \sum_{k=1}^{n} |\sin (\pi k! x)|^{1/n} \) consists of terms that approach 1 if \( |\sin (\pi k! x)| \neq 0 \) and 0 if \( |\sin (\pi k! x)| = 0 \).
   - For the limit to be bounded, the number of terms that approach 1 must be finite.

3. **Rational \( x \)**:
   - Suppose \( x = \frac{p}{q} \) where \( p \) and \( q \) are integers and \( q > 0 \).
   - For \( k \geq q \), \( k! \) is divisible by \( q \), so \( k! x = k! \frac{p}{q} \) is an integer.
   - Therefore, \( |\sin (\pi k! x)| = |\sin (\pi \cdot \text{integer})| = 0 \) for \( k \geq q \).
   - The sum \( \sum_{k=1}^{n} |\sin (\pi k! x)|^{1/n} \) reduces to \( \sum_{k=1}^{q-1} |\sin (\pi k! x)|^{1/n} \) for \( n \geq q \).
   - As \( n \to \infty \), each term \( |\sin (\pi k! x)|^{1/n} \to 1 \) for \( k < q \), so the sum approaches \( q-1 \), which is finite.

4. **Irrational \( x \)**:
   - If \( x \) is irrational, \( k! x \) is never an integer for any \( k \).
   - Therefore, \( |\sin (\pi k! x)| \) is always positive and less than 1.
   - As \( n \to \infty \), each term \( |\sin (\pi k! x)|^{1/n} \to 1 \).
   - The sum \( \sum_{k=1}^{n} |\sin (\pi k! x)|^{1/n} \) approaches \( n \), which goes to infinity.

### Conclusion

The limit \( \lim_{n \to \infty} \sum_{k=1}^{n} |\sin (\pi k! x)|^{1/n} \) is bounded if and only if \( x \) is rational. Therefore, the set of all \( x \) such that the limit is bounded is the set of rational numbers \( \mathbb{Q} \).

Wait,<#sep#>Add support for report generator plugins that process modified files as a batch, rather than individually
SQLFluff implements the `diff-quality` plugin protocol. SQLFluff users have requested that the `diff-quality` integration should take advantage of SQLFluff's multiprocessing capability (processing files in parallel across multiple processes).

In order to do this, `BaseReportGenerator._diff_violations()` would need to be modified to call a different function if provided by the plugin, e.g. `violations_batch()` rather than `violations()`. If `violations_batch()` is not implemented, fall back to using `violations()`.

I'm happy to provide a PR to implement this, but wanted to ask if this is a reasonable feature and if there are any concerns with the proposed implementation. 🙏🏽

r, c, n, k = map(int, input().split())

grid = [[0] * (c + 1) for _ in range(r + 1)]
for _ in range(n):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    grid[x][y] = 1

prefix = [[0] * (c + 1) for _ in range(r + 1)]
for i in range(1, r + 1):
    for j in range(1, c + 1):
        prefix[i][j] = grid[i][j] + prefix[i - 1][j] + prefix[i][j - 1] - prefix[i - 1][j - 1]

count = 0
for x1 in range(1, r + 1):
    for x2 in range(x1, r + 1):
        for y1 in range(1, c + 1):
            for y2 in range(y1, c + 1):
                total = prefix[x2][y2] - prefix[x1 - 1][y2] - prefix[x2][y1 - 1] + prefix[x1 - 1][y1 - 1]
                if total >= k:
                    count += 1

**Problem description**
I have discovered a bug in the diff-cover library. When an untracked binary file is present in the repository, the library fails to read it and throws the following error:
```
  File "/Users/user/PycharmProjects/some-project/.direnv/python-3.12.3/lib/python3.12/site-packages/diff_cover/diff_reporter.py", line 172, in src_paths_changed
    num_lines = len(file_handle.readlines())
                    ^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^
  File "<frozen codecs>", line 322, in decode
UnicodeDecodeError: 'utf-8' codec can't decode byte 0xb3 in position 0: invalid start byte
```

**Steps to Reproduce:**
1. Create a new repository or use an existing one.
2. Add a binary file to the repository that is not tracked by the version control system (untracked file).
3. Run diff-cover on this repository with `--include-untracked` flag

**Environment:**
diff-cover version: 6.5.1, 9.1.0
Python version: 3.12
Operating System: mac os [Sonomna 14.5]

**Additional Information:**
The problem is likely related to attempting to read all untracked.  It might be helpful to use the mechanism from 
```python
self._git_diff()
```
 that checks 
 ```python
 self._is_path_excluded(src_path) 
 ```
 and:
 ```python
 if (
    not self._supported_extensions
    or extension in self._supported_extensions
):
 ```

This will ensure that only supported file types are read, preventing the UnicodeDecodeError when encountering binary files.

+    def _validate_path_to_diff(self, src_path: str) -> bool:
+        """
+        Validate if a path should be included in the diff.
+
+        Returns True if the path should be included, otherwise False.
+
+        A path should be excluded if:
+        - If the path is excluded
+        - If the path has an extension that is not supported
+        """
+
+        if self._is_path_excluded(src_path):
+            return False
+
+        # If no _supported_extensions provided, or extension present: process
+        _, extension = os.path.splitext(src_path)
+        extension = extension[1:].lower()
+
+        if self._supported_extensions and extension not in self._supported_extensions:
+            return False
+
+        return True

def fwht(a, invert):
    n = len(a)
    h = 1
    while h < n:
        for i in range(0, n, h * 2):
            for j in range(i, i + h):
                x = a[j]
                y = a[j + h]
                a[j] = x + y
                a[j + h] = x - y
        h *= 2
    if invert:
        for i in range(n):
            a[i] = a[i] // n

n, m = map(int, input().split())
rows = [input().strip() for _ in range(n)]
size = 1 << n

count = [0] * size
for j in range(m):
    mask = 0
    for i in range(n):
        if rows[i][j] == '1':
            mask |= 1 << (n - 1 - i)
    count[mask] += 1

f = [0] * size
for mask in range(size):
    cnt = bin(mask).count('1')
    f[mask] = min(cnt, n - cnt)

fwht(count, invert=False)
fwht(f, invert=False)

conv = [count[i] * f[i] for i in range(size)]

fwht(conv, invert=True)

print(min(conv))<#sep#> + y² = m² + n².

<image> Duck is the ultimate animal! (Image courtesy of See Bang)

The journey can be represented as a straight line, consisting of n segments. Bob is located to the left of the first segment, while Alice's nest is on the right of the last segment. Each segment has a length in meters, and also terrain type: grass, water or lava. 

Bob has three movement types: swimming, walking and flying. He can switch between them or change his direction at any point in time (even when he is located at a non-integer coordinate), and doing so doesn't require any extra time. Bob can swim only on the water, walk only on the grass and fly over any terrain. Flying one meter takes 1 second, swimming one meter takes 3 seconds, and finally walking one meter takes 5 seconds.

Bob has a finite amount of energy, called stamina. Swimming and walking is relaxing for him, so he gains 1 stamina for every meter he walks or swims. On the other hand, flying is quite tiring, and he spends 1 stamina for every meter flown. Staying in place does not influence his stamina at all. Of course, his stamina can never become negative. Initially, his stamina is zero.

What is the shortest possible time in which he can reach Alice's nest?
Input

The first line contains a single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^5) — the number of segments of terrain. 

The second line contains n integers l_1, l_2, ..., l_n (1 ≤ l_i ≤ 10^{12}). The l_i represents the length of the i-th terrain segment in meters.

The third line contains a string s consisting of n characters "G", "W", "L", representing Grass, Water and Lava, respectively. 

It is guaranteed that the first segment is not Lava.

Output

Output a single integer t — the minimum time Bob needs to reach Alice. 

Examples

Input


1
10
G


Output


30


Input


2
10 10
WL


Output


40


Input


2
1 2
WL


Output


8


Input


3
10 10 10
GLW


Output


80

Note

In the first sample, Bob first walks 5 meters in 25 seconds. Then he flies the remaining 5 meters in 5 seconds.

In the second sample, Bob first swims 10 meters in 30 seconds. Then he flies over the patch of lava for 10 seconds.

In the third sample, the water pond is much smaller. Bob first swims over the water pond, taking him 3 seconds. However, he cannot fly over the lava just yet, as he only has one stamina while he needs two. So he swims back for half a meter, and then half a meter forward, taking him 3 seconds in total. Now he has 2 stamina, so he can spend 2 seconds flying over the lava.

In the fourth sample, he walks for 50 seconds, flies for 10 seconds, swims for 15 seconds, and finally flies for 5 seconds.

gpt: <think> Okay, so I need to figure out the minimal time Bob the duck needs to get across all these terrain segments. Let me think through this problem step by step. 

First, the problem gives n terrain segments. Each has a length and a type: grass (G), water (W), or lava (L). Bob can move by swimming, walking, or flying. Each movement type has different time costs and stamina changes. 

The key points here are:

- Flying costs stamina (1 per meter), but can be used on any terrain.
- Walking (grass) and swimming (water) give +1 stamina per meter (since they're relaxing). They also take more time (5 and 3 seconds per meter compared to flying's 1 second).
- Bob starts with 0 stamina. 

The goal is to find the minimal time path where Bob can't go below 0 stamina at any point.

<#sep#>user: I am writing a Kivy mobile web app, using python. I want to create a explosion of particles (circles) when the user clicks anywhere on the screen. 
When the user clicks or touches down 10-20  Particles of class Widget get created and added to a list in the Game class, at that position and given random velocities. Use Clock.schedule_event(self.update) to loop through the particles and update their position.

assistant: To create an explosion of particles in a Kivy mobile web app using Python, you can follow these steps:

1. Create a Particle class that inherits from the Widget class. This class should have properties such as position, velocity, and size.
2. In the Game class, create a list to store the particles that will be created when the user clicks the screen.
3. Create a function that will handle the click event. This function should create 10-20 instances of the Particle class at the position of the click, with random velocities.
4. Add the particles to the list created in step 2.
5. Create a function that will update the position of the particles. This function should loop through the list of particles and update their position based on their velocity.
6. Use the Clock.schedule_interval() function to call the update function at a set interval.

Here's some sample code to get you started:

```python
from kivy.app import App
from kivy.uix.widget import Widget
from kivy.graphics import Color, Ellipse
from kivy.clock import Clock
import random

class Particle(Widget):
    velocity_x = 0
    velocity_y = 0

    def update(self):
        self.pos = (self.pos[0] + self.velocity_x, self.pos[1] + self.velocity_y)

class Game(Widget):
    particles = []

    def on_touch_down(self, touch):
        for i in range(random.randint(10, 20)):
            particle = Particle(pos=touch.pos)
            particle.velocity_x = random.uniform(-10, 10)
            particle.velocity_y = random.uniform(-10, 10)
            self.particles.append(particle)
            self.add_widget(particle)

    def update(self, dt):
        for particle in self.particles:
            particle.update()

class MyApp(App):
    def build(self):
        game = Game()
        Clock.schedule_interval(game.update, 1.0 / 60.0)
        return game

if __name__ == '__main__':
    MyApp().run()
```

user: How can I customize the appearance of the particles? Can I change their color, size, or shape?

assistant: Yes, you can customize the appearance of the particles by modifying the `Particle` class. To change the color, size, or shape, you can use Kivy's canvas instructions within the `Particle` class. Here's an example of how to achieve this:

1. Import the necessary classes from `kivy.graphics`:
```python
from kivy.graphics import Ellipse, Rectangle, Line
```
Modify the `Particle` class to include a `canvas` instruction. You can set the particle's color, size, and shape within the `with self.canvas`: block:
```python
class Particle(Widget):
    velocity_x = 0
    velocity_y = 0

    def __init__(self, **kwargs):
        super(Particle, self).__init__(**kwargs)
        self.size = (random.randint(10, 40), random.randint(10, 40))  # Set the size of the particle
        with self.canvas:
            Color(random.random(), random.random(), random.random(), 1)  # Set the color of the particle
            # Choose one of the shapes below:
            Ellipse(pos=self.pos, size=self.size)  # Circular shape
            # Rectangle(pos=self.pos, size=self.size)  # Rectangular shape
            # Line(points=[self.x, self.y, self.x + self.width, self.y + self.height], width=2)  # Line shape

    def update(self):
        self.pos = (self.pos[0] + self.velocity_x, self.pos[1] + self.velocity_y)
```

In the example above, I've set the particle's size randomly within a specified range, assigned a random color, and provided three different shapes (Ellipse, Rectangle, and Line) that you can choose from. You can uncomment the desired shape and comment out the others.

Now, when you run the application, the particles should have customized appearances based on the modifications you made to the `Particle` class.<#sep#>-

import sys
from collections import deque

def main():
    n, m = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    degrees = [0] * (n + 1)
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
        degrees[u] += 1
        degrees[v] += 1
    
    S = [i for i in range(1, n+1) if degrees[i] > 0]
    color = [-1] * (n + 1)
    possible = True
    
    for u in S:
        if color[u] == -1:
            queue = deque()
            queue.append(u)
            color[u] = 0
            while queue and possible:
                current = queue.popleft()
                for v in adj[current]:
                    if degrees[v] == 0:
                        continue
                    if color[v] == -1:
                        color[v] = color[current] ^ 1
                        queue.append(v)
                    elif color[v] == color[current]:
                        possible = False
                        break
                if not possible:
                    break
        if not possible:
            break
    
    if not possible:
        print(-1)
    else:
        A = [u for u in S if color[u] == 0]
        B = [u for u in S if color[u] == 1]
        print(len(A))
        print(' '.join(map(str, A)))
        print(len(B))
        print(' '.join(map(str, B)))

<#sep#>user: Explica como funcionan las bombas de agua.

assistant: Las bombas de agua son dispositivos mecánicos que se utilizan para transferir agua de un lugar a otro, con el objetivo de presionar y aumentar el flujo de agua.

El funcionamiento de una bomba de agua se basa en la ley de acción y reacción de Newton, también conocida como la tercera ley de Newton, que establece que para cada acción hay una reacción igual y opuesta. En el caso de las bombas de agua, la acción es el movimiento del eje de la bomba, que genera una reacción en el fluido que se encuentra dentro de la bomba.

Hay varios tipos de bombas de agua, pero en general todas funcionan de manera similar. Primero, el motor o el motor eléctrico hace girar el eje de la bomba. El eje está conectado a un rotor o una carcasa, que es el componente que se mueve y genera el flujo de agua. El rotor o la carcasa se encuentra dentro de una carcasa estacionaria, que contiene el fluido.

El movimiento del rotor o de la carcasa hace que el fluido se mueva hacia la salida de la bomba, aumentando su presión y su flujo. Este movimiento hace que la presión en la entrada de la bomba sea menor que la presión en la salida, creando una diferencia de presión. La diferencia de presión es la fuerza que impulsa el fluido a través de la bomba y a su destino final.

Una vez que el fluido ha sido transferido a su destino, la bomba vuelve a repetir el proceso, manteniendo un flujo constante de fluido.

En resumen, las bombas de agua funcionan convirtiendo la energía mecánica del motor o del motor eléctrico en energía hidráulica, aumentando la presión y el flujo de agua, y permitiendo la transferencia del agua de un lugar a otro.

user: La respuesta parece confusa. Ahí solamente están reflejadas las bombas centrífugas. ¿Me puedes dar un ejemplo de una bomba de desplazamiento positivo? ¿Me podrías dar un ejemplo de curva característica de una bomba?

assistant: Si claro, aqui tienes: 

Un ejemplo de una bomba de desplazamiento positivo es una bomba de pistón. La bomba de pistón funciona mediante un émbolo o un pistón que se desplaza dentro de un cilindro. Cada vez que el émbolo se desplaza hacia adentro, una cámara se llena con el fluido, y cada vez que se desplaza hacia afuera, el fluido es expulsado de la bomba.

La curva característica de una bomba describe cómo varía la capacidad de la bomba con respecto a la presión. En otras palabras, la curva característica muestra cómo cambia el caudal de la bomba a medida que aumenta la presión. La curva característica se representa gráficamente en una carta con caudal en el eje horizontal y presión en el eje vertical. La forma de la curva característica depende de la geometría de la bomba, de la velocidad de giro y de la viscosidad del fluido.<#sep#>-

n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
current_c = 0

for i in range(n):
    ai = a[i]
    total_available = ai - current_c
    if total_available < 0:
        print("NO")
        exit()
    if i == n - 1:
        max_possible = 0
    else:
        next_ai = a[i + 1]
        max_possible = min(total_available, next_ai)
    desired_par = total_available % 2

    # Compute c_i
    if max_possible % 2 == desired_par:
        c_i = max_possible
    else:
        if max_possible >= 1:
            c_i = max_possible - 1
        else:
            print("NO")
            exit()
    
    # Check validity of c_i
    if c_i < 0 or c_i % 2 != desired_par or c_i > max_possible:
        print("NO")
        exit()
    
    current_c = c_i

<#sep#>user: Quantfy the effect of average caffeine intake from coffee and caffeinated drinks on Western economies. Explain the data sources and methods you use to arrive at your conclusion.

assistant: According to a report by the National Coffee Association, consumers spent $74.2 billion on coffee in 2015 in the United States, and the coffee industry was responsible for 1,694,710 jobs and nearly $28 billion in taxes. The average caffeine intake from coffee was about 165 mg per day per person. For other caffeinated drinks, such as tea, soda and energy drinks, the caffeine content varies widely, but according to the Mayo Clinic, the average intake from these sources was about 51 mg per day per person. So, the total average caffeine intake from coffee and caffeinated drinks in the US was about 216 mg per day per person.

Caffeine is a stimulant that can enhance alertness, mood, cognitive performance and physical endurance. It can also block the effects of adenosine, a hormone that is responsible for deep sleep. These effects can have positive or negative consequences depending on the dose, timing and individual sensitivity. For example, moderate caffeine intake (up to 400 mg per day) can decrease the risk of several cancers, cardiovascular disease, type 2 diabetes and Parkinson’s disease. It can also improve work efficiency, learning ability and problem-solving skills. However, excessive caffeine intake (more than 400 mg per day) or consumption late in the day can cause insomnia, anxiety, irritability, headaches, dehydration and dependence. It can also impair memory consolidation, emotional regulation and creativity.

Some researchers have attempted to estimate the economic value of caffeine using different methods and assumptions. For instance, a study by Griffiths et al. used a simulation model to calculate the net benefit of caffeine consumption in the US workforce based on productivity gains and health care savings minus productivity losses and health care costs. They found that the net benefit was $74.4 billion per year. Another study by Pollan used a historical perspective to argue that caffeine consumption drove a kind of “Enlightenment thinking” that fostered innovation, rationality and economic growth in Western societies.

The profit margin for coffee shops ranges between 2.5% and 8.79%, depending on whether they roast their own coffee or not. These numbers indicate that the coffee industry is more profitable than the food processing industry.

The gross profit margin for the food processing industry was 22.05% in 2019, which was considerably below the overall market average of 49.4%. The EBITDA margin for food processing was 9.56%, and the net profit margin was 5.16%. These figures suggest that the food processing industry is not very profitable compared to other sectors.

However, profitability may vary depending on the type of product, location, competition, and customer preferences. Some other food businesses that have high profit margins are honey production (30%), popcorn business (22%), custom cakes (19%), and pizza (15%).<#sep#>-

import sys

MOD = 10**9 + 7

def euler_phi(n):
    if n == 1:
        return 1
    result = n
    i = 2
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i == 0:
            while n % i == 0:
                n //= i
            result -= result // i
        i += 1
    if n > 1:
        result -= result // n
    return result

def main():
    n, k = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())
    current = n
    steps = 0
    while steps < k and current != 1:
        current = euler_phi(current)
        steps += 1
    print(current % MOD)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()<#sep#> of other variables. For example, let’s set x = p + q and y = p - q, assuming p > q. Then:

<#sep#>Feature request: Optional range parameter for download request
Hi! 

Developer of B2Fuse here. An idea was put forth to use B2 CLI Python API as backend for B2Fuse to handle integration against B2. This is an excellent idea but requires an additional feature from B2 CLI. In order to use B2 CLI Python API in B2Fuse the download call needs to be able to request part of a file. This should be implemented as an optional feature, as in some cases it will also be necessary to request entire files.

Would this be possible to add? 

Best 
Sondre

The resume should be formatted in a clear and easy-to-read manner, with consistent use of bullet points, headings, and whitespace to enhance readability.

Here are some additional formatting guidelines to consider:

1. Use a simple and straightforward resume format, such as chronological or functional, to highlight your skills and experience.

2. Use bold, italics, and capitalization to emphasize important information, such as job titles, company names, and technical skills.

3. Use bullet points to list your professional experience, highlighting your accomplishments and responsibilities in each role.

4. Include a summary or objective statement at the top of your resume to highlight your career goals and key qualifications.

5. Keep the length of your resume to one or two pages, depending on your level of experience and qualifications.

6. Use consistent formatting throughout your resume, including font style, size, and spacing.

7. Avoid using images, graphics, or other visual elements that may distract from the content of your resume.

Overall, the goal of your resume should be to present your skills and experience in a clear, concise, and professional manner that highlights your qualifications for the software development job at Amazon.<#sep#>-

s = input().strip()
original = 0
n = len(s)
for i in range(n - 1):
    if s[i] == 'V' and s[i+1] == 'K':
        original += 1

max_count = original

for i in range(n):
    temp = list(s)
    temp[i] = 'K' if temp[i] == 'V' else 'V'
    modified = ''.join(temp)
    count = 0
    for j in range(len(modified) - 1):
        if modified[j] == 'V' and modified[j+1] == 'K':
            count += 1
    if count > max_count:
        max_count = count

Let me compute each term:

First term: (u² + 2uv + v²)/4

Second term: (u² - v²)/4

Third term: (u² - 2uv + v²)/4

Adding them up:

[(u² + 2uv + v²) + (u² - v²) + (u² - 2uv + v²)] /4

= [3u² + 0uv + v²]/4

= (3u² + v²)/4

= [(u + v)/2]^2 - [(u + v)/2][(u - v)/2] + [(u - v)/2]^2

= (u² + 3v²)/4

n = int(input())
commands = [input().split() for _ in range(2 * n)]

current_k = 1
reorders = 0
stack = []
max_stack = []

for cmd in commands:
    if cmd[0] == 'add':
        x = int(cmd[1])
        stack.append(x)
        if not max_stack or x > max_stack[-1]:
            max_stack.append(x)
        else:
            max_stack.append(max_stack[-1])
    else:
        if stack and stack[-1] == current_k:
            stack.pop()
            max_stack.pop()
            current_k += 1
        else:
            reorders += 1
            if stack:
                current_k = max_stack[-1] + 1
                stack.clear()
                max_stack.clear()

<#sep#>user: How do I instantiate a scene in the Godot 3.5 game engine?

assistant: To instantiate a scene in Godot 3.5 game engine, you can follow these steps:

1. Create the scene you want to instantiate as a separate .tscn file.
2. In the scene where you want to instantiate the other scene, add a Node2D or other node that will serve as the parent for the instantiated scene.
3. Select the parent node and click the "Add Child Node" button in the bottom right corner of the screen.
4. In the "Create Node" dialog, select "Load" and navigate to the .tscn file of the scene you want to instantiate.
5. Select the .tscn file and click "Load."
6. The instantiated scene will now appear in the scene tree as a child of the parent node you selected.

You can now customize the instantiated scene as needed. You can also use scripting to dynamically instantiate scenes at runtime using the <code>load</code> and <code>instance</code> methods. For example:

    # Load the scene as a PackedScene
    var scene = load("res://scenes/MyScene.tscn")
    
    # Instance the scene as a Node
    var instance = scene.instance()
    
    # Add the instance as a child of the current node
    add_child(instance)

Note that you'll need to replace "res://scenes/MyScene.tscn" with the path to your own scene file.

user: Thank you this was really helpful.

assistant: No problem! Glad it helped. I can  help you with anything else if you have any questions, such as how to create a new scene from scratch.

user: I'm currently making a 2d platform, how would I go about making a menu that contains an inventory, and a list of how many enemies destroyed and how many lives are left.

assistant: To create a menu with an inventory, enemy count, and lives count in a 2D platformer using Godot, follow these steps:

1. Create a new scene for the menu:
   - Click on "Scene" > "New Scene" in the top-left corner.
   - Add a "CanvasLayer" node as the root node. This will ensure that the menu is drawn on top of the game scene.
   - Save the scene as "Menu.tscn" or another name of your choice.

2. Design the menu layout:
   - Add a "Control" node as a child of the "CanvasLayer" node. This will serve as a container for the menu elements.
   - Add a "VBoxContainer" node as a child of the "Control" node. This will automatically arrange the menu elements vertically.
   - Add "Label" nodes as children of the "VBoxContainer" node for each menu element (inventory, enemy count, and lives count). Name them accordingly (e.g., "InventoryLabel", "EnemyCountLabel", "LivesCountLabel").

3. Create a script for the menu:
   - Select the "Control" node and click the "Attach Script" button in the top-right corner.
   - Choose a name and location for the script (e.g., "Menu.gd") and click "Create".
   - In the script, create variables to store the references to the "Label" nodes:

```gdscript
onready var inventory_label = $VBoxContainer/InventoryLabel
onready var enemy_count_label = $VBoxContainer/EnemyCountLabel
onready var lives_count_label = $VBoxContainer/LivesCountLabel
```

4. Update the menu with game data:
   - In the script, create a function to update the menu with the current inventory, enemy count, and lives count. You can pass these values as arguments to the function:

```gdscript
func update_menu(inventory: Array, enemy_count: int, lives_count: int):
    inventory_label.text = "Inventory: " + ", ".join([str(item) for item in inventory])
    enemy_count_label.text = "Enemies Destroyed: " + str(enemy_count)
    lives_count_label.text = "Lives Left: " + str(lives_count)
```

5. Instantiate the menu in the main game scene:
   - Open your main game scene (e.g., "Game.tscn").
   - Add a "CanvasLayer" node to the scene if you don't already have one.
   - Instance the "Menu.tscn" scene as a child of the "CanvasLayer" node.
   - In the main game script, create a reference to the menu instance:

```gdscript
onready var menu = $CanvasLayer/Menu
```

6. Update the menu with game data:
   - Whenever the inventory, enemy count, or lives count changes in your game, call the `update_menu` function on the menu instance and pass the updated values:

```gdscript
menu.update_menu(inventory, enemy_count, lives_count)
```

Now you have a basic menu that displays the inventory, enemy count, and lives count in your 2D platformer. You can further customize the appearance and functionality of the menu as needed.<#sep#>-

def construct_bracket_sequence(n, k):
    max_sum = n * (n - 1) // 2
    if k > max_sum:
        return "Impossible"
    if k == 0:
        return "()" * n

    m = 0
    while (m + 1) * m // 2 <= k:
        m += 1
    m -= 1
    r = k - m * (m - 1) // 2
    available = (n - m) * m
    if r > available:
        return "Impossible"
    
    a = r
    b = (n - m) - a // m if m != 0 else 0
    a_part = a // m if m != 0 else 0
    remainder = a % m

    # Build the sequence
    res = []
    res.append('(' * m)
    if m > 0:
        res.append('()' * a_part)
        if remainder > 0:
            res.append('(' * remainder)
            res.append(')' * remainder)
        remaining = (n - m) - a_part - (remainder > 0)
        res.append(')' * m)
        res.append('()' * remaining)
    else:
        res.append('()' * n)
    return ''.join(res) if len(''.join(res)) == 2 * n else "Impossible"

n, k = map(int, input().split())
result = construct_bracket_sequence(n, k)
print(result)<#sep#>, t² -t +1=1, square. Similarly, t=2: t² +t +1=7, not square.

(1/4) + (1/2) +1=1.75, not square.

t=3/2:

(9/4)+(3/2)+1= (9/4 +6/4 +4/4)=19/4, not square.

t=5/4:

(25/16)+(5/4)+1=25/16 +20/16 +16/16=61/16, not square.

t= sqrt(3): irrational.

It seems like even in rational numbers, there are very few solutions. Perhaps the only solution is t=1, which gives the previous case x=y=1, but that leads to first equation 3, not square.

</think>To solve the problem of finding all positive integers \( x \) and \( y \) such that both \( x^2 + xy + y^2 \) and \( x^2 - xy + y^2 \) are perfect squares, we will proceed systematically.

Let:
\[ A = x^2 + xy + y^2 \]
\[ B = x^2 - xy + y^2 \]

We need \( A \) and \( B \) to be perfect squares. Let:
\[ A = a^2 \]
\[ B = b^2 \]

First, consider the difference:
\[ A - B = (x^2 + xy + y^2) - (x^2 - xy + y^2) = 2xy \]
Thus:
\[ a^2 - b^2 = 2xy \]
\[ (a - b)(a + b) = 2xy \]

Since \( a \) and \( b \) are integers, \( a - b \) and \( a + b \) must be factors of \( 2xy \). Also, note that \( a - b \) and \( a + b \) must have the same parity (both even or both odd). Since \( 2xy \) is even, both \( a - b \) and \( a + b \) must be even.

Let:
\[ a - b = 2m \]
\[ a + b = 2n \]

Then:
\[ (2m)(2n) = 2xy \]
\[ 4mn = 2xy \]
\[ mn = \frac{xy}{2} \]

Since \( mn \) is an integer, \( xy \) must be even. Therefore, at least one of \( x \) or \( y \) must be even.

Next, consider the sum:
\[ A + B = (x^2 + xy + y^2) + (x^2 - xy + y^2) = 2x^2 + 2y^2 \]
\[ a^2 + b^2 = 2x^2 + 2y^2 \]
\[ \frac{a^2 + b^2}{2} = x^2 + y^2 \]

We now have the system:
1. \( mn = \frac{xy}{2} \)
2. \( x^2 + y^2 = m^2 + n^2 \)

To find specific solutions, we can test small values of \( x \) and \( y \) to see if they satisfy both conditions.

### Testing Small Values

#### Case \( x = 1 \)
\[ A = 1 + y + y^2 \]
\[ B = 1 - y + y^2 \]

For \( y = 1 \):
\[ A = 1 + 1 + 1 = 3 \quad (\text{not a perfect square}) \]
\[ B = 1 - 1 + 1 = 1 \quad (\text{perfect square}) \]

For \( y = 2 \):
\[ A = 1 + 2 + 4 = 7 \quad (\text{not a perfect square}) \]
\[ B = 1 - 2 + 4 = 3 \quad (\text{not a perfect square}) \]

#### Case \( x = 2 \)
\[ A = 4 + 2y + y^2 \]
\[ B = 4 - 2y + y^2 \]

For \( y = 1 \):
\[ A = 4 + 2 + 1 = 7 \quad (\text{not a perfect square}) \]
\[ B = 4 - 2 + 1 = 3 \quad (\text{not a perfect square}) \]

For \( y = 2 \):
\[ A = 4 + 4 + 4 = 12 \quad (\text{not a perfect square}) \]
\[ B = 4 - 4 + 4 = 4 \quad (\text{perfect square}) \]

#### Case \( x = 3 \)
\[ A = 9 + 3y + y^2 \]
\[ B = 9 - 3y + y^2 \]

For \( y = 1 \):
\[ A = 9 + 3 + 1 = 13 \quad (\text{not a perfect square}) \]
\[ B = 9 - 3 + 1 = 7 \quad (\text{not a perfect square}) \]

For \( y = 2 \):
\[ A = 9 + 6 + 4 = 19 \quad (\text{not a perfect square}) \]
\[ B = 9 - 6 + 4 = 7 \quad (\text{not a perfect square}) \]

For \( y = 3 \):
\[ A = 9 + 9 + 9 = 27 \quad (\text{not a perfect square}) \]
\[ B = 9 - 9 + 9 = 9 \quad (\text{perfect square}) \]

### General Analysis

From the above tests, it is evident that no small values of \( x \) and \( y \) satisfy both conditions simultaneously. Extending this analysis to larger values or using more advanced number theory techniques would similarly show no solutions.

2. Find the points where we<#sep#>support for bucket info and lifecycle rules
The B2 service now supports storing bucket info with a bucket, and will soon support setting lifecycle rules to automatically delete old versions of files.

Any thoughts on what the interface to the command-line tool should be?

The obvious thing to do is to add `--bucket-info` and `--lifecycle-rules` options to the `create_bucket` and `update_bucket` operations.  Each would take JSON as an argument, like this:

```
b2 update-bucket --bucket-info '{"color": "blue"}' my-bucket
```

That seems reasonable, but may be inconvenient for storing large bucket info or a long list of lifecycle rules.  We could support pulling from a file, perhaps by using the curl convention of "@" to indicate a file.  (That will be unambiguous because JSON can't start with "@".)

```
b2 update-bucket --lifecycle-rules @rules.json my-bucket
```

Does this sound reasonable?

assistant: The human visual system is indeed remarkable in its ability to see a wide range of colors. While it is true that the retina in the human eye contains only three types of color-sensitive cells, called cone cells, these cells are capable of responding to different wavelengths of light, and by combining their signals, the brain is able to perceive a wide range of colors.

The three types of cone cells are sensitive to different parts of the electromagnetic spectrum: short (S) wavelengths, which correspond to blue light; medium (M) wavelengths, which correspond to green light; and long (L) wavelengths, which correspond to red light. By combining the signals from these three types of cone cells, our brains are able to perceive colors across the entire visible spectrum.

So, you might wonder, how do we see colors that aren't simply combinations of blue, green, and red light? The answer lies in the way that our brains interpret these signals. When we perceive a yellow object, for example, the object is reflecting light with a specific pattern of wavelengths, which stimulates both our medium (green) and long (red) wavelength-sensitive cone cells. Our brains interpret this combination of signals as the color yellow.

Similarly, when we see colors like magenta, cyan, and other colors that aren't directly represented in the spectrum of light, our brains are interpreting a combination of signals from different types of cone cells to produce the perception of that color.

This phenomenon is known as color vision, and it is a complex and fascinating topic in the field of vision science.

user: What type of vision for the future will people have?

assistant: That’s an interesting question. There are different possible scenarios for the future of human vision, depending on the factors that influence it, such as evolution, technology, and environment. Some possible scenarios are:

Future humans will have larger brains and eyes, as a result of adapting to dimmer and more distant environments, such as outer space. This would allow them to process more information and see better in low-light conditions1.
Future humans will have bionic eyes, as a result of improving technology that can restore or enhance vision for people with vision loss. Bionic eyes are devices that use electrodes to stimulate the retina or the brain, creating artificial visual signals23.
Future humans will have enhanced vision, as a result of using wearable or implantable devices that can augment the natural vision with additional features, such as zooming, filtering, or displaying information. This would allow them to see beyond the normal limits of human vision4.
Of course, these are just some speculative possibilities, and the actual future of human vision may be different or a combination of these scenarios.<#sep#>-

import bisect

n = int(input())
q = int(input())
queries = [tuple(map(int, input().split())) for _ in range(q)]

intervals = []
total_modified = 0
total_working = 0

for l, r, k in queries:
    new_intervals = []
    overlap = []
    
    # Find all overlapping intervals
    # Using bisect to find the first interval with start > r
    idx = bisect.bisect_right(intervals, (r + 1,)) - 1
    while idx >= 0 and intervals[idx][1] >= l:
        interval = intervals[idx]
        if interval[0] > r:
            idx -= 1
            continue
        # Check if overlap
        if interval[1] >= l:
            overlap.append(interval)
            del intervals[idx]
        idx -= 1
    
    # Process overlapping intervals
    for start, end, status in overlap:
        # Subtract from totals
        length = end - start + 1
        total_modified -= length
        if status == 2:
            total_working -= length
        
        # Split into left, middle, right
        left_start = start
        left_end = min(end, l - 1)
        if left_start <= left_end:
            bisect.insort(intervals, (left_start, left_end, status))
            len_left = left_end - left_start + 1
            total_modified += len_left
            if status == 2:
                total_working += len_left
        
        right_start = max(start, r + 1)
        right_end = end
        if right_start <= right_end:
            bisect.insort(intervals, (right_start, right_end, status))
            len_right = right_end - right_start + 1
            total_modified += len_right
            if status == 2:
                total_working += len_right
    
    # Add new interval
    new_start = l
    new_end = r
    new_k = k
    bisect.insort(intervals, (new_start, new_end, new_k))
    len_new = new_end - new_start + 1
    total_modified += len_new
    if new_k == 2:
        total_working += len_new
    
    # Merge with adjacent intervals if possible
    pos = bisect.bisect_left(intervals, (new_start,)) 
    # Check left
    if pos > 0:
        left = intervals[pos-1]
        if left[1] == new_start -1 and left[2] == new_k:
            merged_start = left[0]
            merged_end = new_end
            # Remove left and current
            del intervals[pos-1]
            del intervals[pos-1]
            bisect.insort(intervals, (merged_start, merged_end, new_k))
            # Update totals
            total_modified -= (left[1] - left[0] + 1) + len_new
            total_modified += merged_end - merged_start + 1
            if new_k == 2:
                total_working -= (left[1] - left[0] + 1) + len_new
                total_working += merged_end - merged_start + 1
            new_start = merged_start
            new_end = merged_end
    # Check right
    if pos < len(intervals):
        right = intervals[pos]
        if right[0] == new_end + 1 and right[2] == new_k:
            merged_start = new_start
            merged_end = right[1]
            # Remove current and right
            if pos < len(intervals):
                del intervals[pos]
            if pos < len(intervals):
                del intervals[pos]
            bisect.insort(intervals, (merged_start, merged_end, new_k))
            # Update totals
            total_modified -= (new_end - new_start + 1) + (right[1] - right[0] + 1)
            total_modified += merged_end - merged_start + 1
            if new_k == 2:
                total_working -= (new_end - new_start + 1) + (right[1] - right[0] + 1)
                total_working += merged_end - merged_start + 1
    
    current_total = total_working + (n - total_modified)
    print(current_total)<#sep#> 4 = 7 \quad (\text{not a perfect square}) \]

### General Analysis

### Conclusion

+    def update_bucket<#sep#>user: What are the main differences between Meiosis and Mitosis in human cells?

assistant: Meiosis and mitosis are two different processes of cell division that occur in human cells. While both processes result in the formation of new cells, there are several key differences between them:

Purpose: The main difference between meiosis and mitosis is the purpose of cell division. Meiosis is a type of cell division that produces haploid cells, which have half the number of chromosomes as the parent cell. This process is essential for sexual reproduction, as it allows the formation of gametes (sperm and egg cells) with half the number of chromosomes. On the other hand, mitosis is a type of cell division that results in the formation of diploid cells, which have the same number of chromosomes as the parent cell. Mitosis is responsible for growth, repair, and replacement of damaged or lost cells.

Number of daughter cells: Another difference between meiosis and mitosis is the number of daughter cells produced by each process. Meiosis results in the formation of four haploid daughter cells, while mitosis results in the formation of two identical daughter cells.

Chromosome number: Meiosis involves two rounds of cell division, during which the chromosomes are replicated and then divided. This results in the formation of daughter cells with half the number of chromosomes as the parent cell. In contrast, mitosis involves a single round of cell division, during which the chromosomes are replicated and then divided. The daughter cells produced by mitosis have the same number of chromosomes as the parent cell.

Chromosome distribution: Another difference between meiosis and mitosis is the way in which chromosomes are distributed to the daughter cells. During meiosis, the homologous chromosomes (pairs of chromosomes that carry genes for the same traits) separate from each other and are distributed to different daughter cells. This results in genetic diversity among the daughter cells. During mitosis, the replicated chromosomes are divided evenly between the daughter cells, resulting in identical daughter cells.

Genetic variation: Meiosis results in genetic variation among the daughter cells, due to the random distribution of chromosomes to the daughter cells during cell division. This genetic variation is essential for the survival of species, as it allows for adaptation to changing environments. In contrast, mitosis results in identical daughter cells, as the chromosomes are divided evenly between the daughter cells.

In conclusion, meiosis and mitosis are two different processes of cell division that occur in human cells, with distinct purposes, results, and mechanisms. Understanding the differences between these processes is important for understanding how cells divide and how genetic information is passed on from one generation to the next.

user: Do other plants/animals undergo a different process of cell division?

assistant: Yes, there are various types of cell division processes in different organisms, and the process can vary depending on the species and the purpose of the division. Here are some examples:

1. Binary fission: This is the method of cell division in prokaryotes, such as bacteria. In this process, the cell duplicates its genetic material, and then divides into two identical daughter cells.

2. Budding: This is a form of asexual reproduction in which a new individual develops from an outgrowth or bud on the parent. This process is seen in some single-celled organisms, such as yeast, as well as in some multicellular organisms, such as Hydra.

3. Fragmentation: This is a form of asexual reproduction that occurs in some animals, such as starfish and planarians, where a piece of the organism can break off and grow into a new individual.

4. Vegetative propagation: This is a form of asexual reproduction in plants, in which a new plant grows from a part of the parent plant, such as a stem or a root. Examples include runners, rhizomes, and bulbs.

5. Multiple fission: This is a type of cell division in which a single cell divides into multiple daughter cells. This process is seen in some single-celled organisms, such as the malaria parasite.

So, the process of cell division can vary depending on the organism and the purpose of the division, and can involve different mechanisms and structures.<#sep#>-

s = input().strip()
n = len(s)
best = s
max_k = int(n**0.5)
for k in range(max_k, 0, -1):
    m = n - k * k
    if m < 0:
        continue
    stack = []
    for i, c in enumerate(s):
        while stack and stack[-1] > c and len(stack) + (n - i - 1) >= m:
            stack.pop()
        if len(stack) < m:
            stack.append(c)
    candidate = ''.join(stack[:m])
    if candidate < best:
        best = candidate

 Thus, as long as (next_required — current_stamina) ≤ max_possible_gain_until_next_L:

 Then you can decide what amount to invest in current segment's walk.

 Our goal is to minimize the time.

 Thus, to do that:

 the optimal way is to fly as much as possible now,

 while making sure that the total required_s will be satisfied. 

Thus:

Let the needed = next_required — (current_stamina + rest_max)

 where rest_max is the maximum gain achievable from the remaining non-L segments after current to next_L.

 if needed >0:

 means you have to walk/swim at least needed meters on this current or subsequent non_L segments.

 To achive this, you can walk/swim on this segment as much as possible (maximizing gain to reduce future needed). 

 Thus:

 Use this segment by first satisfying the 'needed' part:

 which is needed: need_to_give = max(0, needed).

 Thus, to cover need_to_give, the required walking/swimming here must be 

 let’s calculate:

 the current segment length is L. 

 the current segment can contribute x meters to gain +x.

 the maximal x possible is L (walk it all).

 Thus:

 the can_give = - (current_stamina + (x + remaing other segmets' maximum) >= needed? )

Hmm I'm really stuck here. 

 Perhaps I shall look for an algorithm that uses the following strategy.

The problem is essentially that we need enough stamina when starting a lava segment, so:

When you reach a starting point of Lava L_i, your S must be ≥ L_i + L_{i+1} + ... 

Thus, the requirement is that before starting any Lava, you must have the required S, which is the savings needed, so.

 We can compute for every Lava segment its required_s = sum of all L's from its first occurrence onward.

 Now, the key is that this required_s must always be satisfied right before that Lava.

 To get this S, the path before it must have enough S.

 Thus, the strategy is: whenever there's a required_s that is yet to be met, we need to collect sufficient S in prior segments.

 Since the path back is freely available (Bob can go back), the best way to collect needed S is on the closest segments before required L.

Thus, for each Lava segment L with required_s:

 if current_s before L is less than required_s:

 need_additional = required_s - current_stamina

 thus you must fly less and walk/swim some distance to gain.

 You can collect S by going back to previous segments and walking/swim those again.

 The minimal time increases, but this is what is needed.

 Thus, how to model this?

 Each time we have a shortage (current_s < required_s) on entering Lava segment, we need to determine how much backtracking must be done. 

 The way to minimize time is to adjust in the neighboring terain as much as possible. 

Let me consider that whenever we reach the start of a Lava segment with insufficient current_S:

 The shortage is delta = required_s - current_S.

 To fix this:

 Bob must replenish delta in the previous segments by retracing path in the previous segments, walking or swimming some of them to gain delta.

 He can choose to do this on any previous non-l segment.

 But the optimal way is to choose the segments with the highest gain per time cost (since we want to cover delta while minimizing time). 

For example, a water segment's swim (gain 1 per meter of walk/swim) consumes 3s per m.

 So per stamina unit gain: takes 3 seconds.

 Walking grass: consumest 5 s.

 Thus, the best is to choose the water's over grass segments for the back and forth cycles because less time per stamina.

 So Bob should choose to loop on water segmentspreferred to get the best (lowest time to S gain) areas. 

 Thus the algorithm might be:

 Keep track of all the previous non-l segments that can be used to gain S, and when a shortage is encountered at a Lava's start, determine how to best use such segments to gain the required S.

 But this leads us to calculate on the fly, wh-involved with recalling past segments and their properties (if they are water or grass). 

Maybe the optimal way is to:

 Whenever you have a shortage delta when starting at Lava L, you must:

 Increase stamina by delta by doing some loops in the previous segments (those prior to L's start), choosing the ones with cheapest time needed to gain a S unit (i.e., water segments (gain per 3 seconds) is better than grass (5)).

 Thus the total extra time needed to aquire delta is delta * min_time_per_unit.

 The min_time_per_unit for the available previous segments. 

 Thus the penalty is delta * t_unit.

 Thus, we can process the paths Normally whenever possible and whenever a deficit is encountered, calculate the required travel time into previous segments to collect the needed S. 

 This would require to keep a record of the total capacity of available 'generating' segments (non-L) before current location, and their utility (efficiency of time per S).

1. Keep track of the iterator through the segments.

2. Have ahead of each Lava segment, calculate its needed required_S, which is sum of Lagas from that point.

3. For each Lava segment (iterated in order), when arriving its start, check if current stamina is ≥ required_S.

4. If yes: fly the L segment. Subtract the length of L from current_s. 

5. If no: compute delta = required_s - current_s.

 Need to make delta more stamina.

 To do that, Collect delta in the most time-efficient way possible.

 For this:

      We need to compute the minimal cost (time) to get delta additions, by using previous non-L segments.

 To do this, we can consider all the non-L segments encountered (before the current L).

 The efficiency (time/Per S) of those segments are:

 For a non-L segment of length L, of type:

 thus per S: 3 seconds. 

 For grass segment:

 walking back and forth gives 2L /5 seconds per S.

<#sep#>user: Write Lua code for the Mudlet application that creates an output window and captures all in-game communications into it

assistant: Sure! Here's an example Lua code for Mudlet that creates an output window and captures all in-game communications into it:

```lua
-- Create a new output window with a given name
local outputWindow = createTextWindow("Game Communications", "Consolas", 12, 0, 0, 400, 300)

-- Define a function to capture all game communications and display them in the output window
function captureGameCommunications(event, message)
  if event == "comm" then
    -- Append the incoming message to the output window
    cecho(outputWindow, "<white>" .. message .. "\n")
  end
end

-- Register the function to capture game communications
registerAnonymousEventHandler("comm", "captureGameCommunications")
```

This code creates a new output window with the name "Game Communications" using the createTextWindow function. Then it defines a function called captureGameCommunications that appends all incoming game communications to the output window using the cecho function. Finally, it registers the function to capture all game communications using the registerAnonymousEventHandler function and the event name "comm".

You can customize the code by modifying the output window properties or the way messages are displayed in the output window. Let me know if you have any questions or need further assistance!

user: How could you modify the Lua code to also capture and display timestamps for each in-game communication message in the output window?

assistant: To capture and display timestamps for each in-game communication message in the output window, you can modify the captureGameCommunications function in the Lua code like this:

```lua
-- Create a new output window with a given name
local outputWindow = createTextWindow("Game Communications", "Consolas", 12, 0, 0, 400, 300)

-- Define a function to capture all game communications and display them in the output window
function captureGameCommunications(event, message)
  if event == "comm" then
    -- Get the current time and format it as a timestamp string
    local time = os.date("%H:%M:%S")
    -- Append the timestamp and incoming message to the output window
    cecho(outputWindow, "<white>" .. time .. " " .. message .. "\n")
  end
end

-- Register the function to capture game communications
registerAnonymousEventHandler("comm", "captureGameCommunications")

```

Here, the os.date function is used to get the current time and format it as a timestamp string with the format "%H:%M:%S" (hour:minute:second). Then, the timestamp and incoming message are appended to the output window using the cecho function.

Wait, but if we consider different triangles where \(R = 1\), then the sides are determined by \(a = 2 \sin A\), \(b = 2 \sin B\), \(c = 2 \sin C\). Therefore, for a given \(R = 1\), the maximum possible \(a\) is 2, when angle \(A\) is 90 degrees. So the answer should be 2.

But wait, let me verify this with the given ratio. The problem states that \(\frac{a^3 + b^3 + c^3}{\sin^3 A + \sin^3 B + \sin^3 C} = 8\). If \(R = 1\), then \(a^3 + b^3 + c^3 = 8 (\sin^3 A + \sin^3 B + \sin^3 C)\), which as we saw earlier, simplifies to \((2R)^3 = 8\), so \(R = 1\). Therefore, for any triangle with \(R = 1\), this ratio holds. Therefore, regardless of the triangle's specific angles, as long as the circumradius is 1, the ratio is 8. Therefore, the problem is just asking, given a triangle with circumradius 1 and sides ordered \(a \geq b \geq c\), what's the maximum possible \(a\). So since \(a = 2 \sin A\), the maximum value of \(\sin A\) is 1, so \(a = 2\). Therefore, the maximum possible length of side \(a\) is 2.

But wait, in this case, is there a triangle with \(R = 1\) and \(a = 2\)? Yes, a right-angled triangle with hypotenuse \(a = 2\), which corresponds to angle \(A = 90^\circ\), and then \(b = 2 \sin B\), \(c = 2 \sin C\). Since \(B + C = 90^\circ\), then \(\sin B\) and \(\sin C\) would be such that \(b\) and \(c\) are both less than 2. For example, if it's isosceles, \(B = C = 45^\circ\), then \(b = c = 2 \times \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2} = \sqrt{2} \approx 1.414\), which is less than 2, satisfying \(a \geq b \geq c\). So that works.

However, what if angle \(A\) is greater than 90 degrees? Then \(\sin A\) would be less than 1, so \(a = 2 \sin A\) would be less than 2, which would make \(a\) smaller. Therefore, the maximum occurs at \(A = 90^\circ\), giving \(a = 2\). Therefore, the maximum length of side \(a\) is 2.

Wait, but before I conclude, let me check if there's any other scenario where \(a\) could be larger. For example, could \(a\) be larger than 2 if the triangle is degenerate? But in a degenerate triangle, the area is zero, and the circumradius would be undefined (since the circumradius formula \(R = \frac{a}{2 \sin A}\) would involve division by zero). But in our problem, the triangle is non-degenerate because the ratio is given as 8, which is finite, so \(R = 1\) is finite, so the triangle must be non-degenerate. Therefore, \(a\) can't be equal to 2 in a degenerate triangle. Therefore, the maximum possible \(a\) is indeed 2.

Alternatively, let's consider if there's another triangle configuration where \(a\) might be larger. Suppose angle \(A\) is less than 90 degrees. Then \(\sin A\) is less than 1, so \(a\) would be less than 2. But in that case, angle \(A\) is not the largest angle, which contradicts the given side ordering \(a \geq b \geq c\). Because if angle \(A\) is less than 90 degrees, then the triangle is acute, and the largest angle is the largest side. Wait, but if angle \(A\) is less than 90 degrees, but \(a\) is still the largest side, then angle \(A\) would actually be the largest angle. Wait, no, in a triangle, the largest side is opposite the largest angle. So if \(a\) is the largest side, then angle \(A\) is the largest angle. So angle \(A\) must be at least as big as angles \(B\) and \(C\). Therefore, if angle \(A\) is less than 90 degrees, then all angles are less than 90 degrees, making the triangle acute. But in that case, \(\sin A\) is still maximized at 90 degrees. So even in acute triangles, the maximum \(a\) would be 2, but angle \(A\) can't exceed 90 degrees if the triangle is acute, so it's not possible. Therefore, the maximum \(a\) is indeed 2.

Therefore, the answer should be \(\boxed{2}\).

**Final Answer**
The maximum length of side \(a\) is \boxed{2}.
</think>Given a triangle \(ABC\) with sides \(a \geq b \geq c\) and angles \(A, B, C\), we are provided with the condition:

\[
\frac{a^3 + b^3 + c^3}{\sin^3 A + \sin^3 B + \sin^3 C} = 8
\]

Using the Law of Sines, we know that:

\[
a = 2R \sin A, \quad b = 2R \sin B, \quad c = 2R \sin C
\]

where \(R\) is the circumradius of the triangle. Substituting these into the given ratio, we get:

\[
\frac{(2R \sin A)^3 + (2R \sin B)^3 + (2R \sin C)^3}{\sin^3 A + \sin^3 B + \sin^3 C} = 8
\]

This simplifies to:

\[
\frac{(2R)^3 (\sin^3 A + \sin^3 B + \sin^3 C)}{\sin^3 A + \sin^3 B + \sin^3 C} = 8
\]

Since \(\sin^3 A + \sin^3 B + \sin^3 C\) is non-zero, we can cancel it out from the numerator and the denominator:

\[
(2R)^3 = 8
\]

Solving for \(R\), we get:

\[
8R^3 = 8 \implies R^3 = 1 \implies R = 1
\]

Thus, the sides of the triangle can be expressed as:

\[
a = 2 \sin A, \quad b = 2 \sin B, \quad c = 2 \sin C
\]

To maximize \(a\), we need to maximize \(\sin A\). The maximum value of \(\sin A\) is 1, which occurs when \(A = 90^\circ\). Therefore:

\[
a = 2 \sin 90^\circ = 2 \times 1 = 2
\]

Given that \(a \geq b \geq c\) and the triangle is non-degenerate, the maximum length of side \(a\) is achieved when the triangle is right-angled at \(A\) with the circumradius \(R = 1\). Thus, the maximum length of side \(a\) is:

\[
\boxed{2}

When you use `sync` to upload files, it always sets `src_last_modified_millis`.  But uploading single files doesn't, which can cause later syncs to get confused.

I propose that `src_last_modified_millis` be set for every uploaded file.  If the user doesn't specify a value on the command line, we can take it from the file.

It would be good to check and make sure that the local clock isn't too different from the server's clock before uploading anything.

<#sep#>user: I was twice as old as my sister when I was 14. Now that my sister is 14, how old am I?

assistant: When you were 14, you were twice as old as your sister, who was 14/2 = 7 years old.
